package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void testAddPositiveIntegers() {
        // If two positive integers are provided as arguments (e.g., 5 and 10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (15). This tests the basic functionality described in 'Returns the sum of a and b'.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 10);
        assertEquals(15, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddNegativeIntegers() {
        // If two negative integers are provided as arguments (e.g., -5 and -10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-15). This ensures the method handles negative numbers correctly as part of the general 'sum' operation.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, -10);
        assertEquals(-15, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMixedSigns() {
        // If one positive integer and one negative integer are provided as arguments (e.g., 10 and -5), then the method should return their algebraic sum (5). This verifies correct handling of mixed signs.
        int result = calculator.add(10, -5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddZeroToZero() {
        // If both arguments are zero, then the method should return 0. This tests the identity element of addition.
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddNonZeroToZero() {
        // If one argument is non-zero (e.g., 5) and the other is zero, then the method should return the non-zero argument (5). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMaxIntegersOverflow() {
        // If both arguments are Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return the result of standard Java integer addition with overflow (specifically -2). Since the documentation does not specify throwing an exception for overflow, the test assumes standard two's complement wrap-around behavior.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMinIntegersOverflow() {
        // If both arguments are Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return the result of standard Java integer addition with overflow (specifically 0). This verifies consistent handling of minimum boundary values under overflow conditions.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMaxPlusOneOverflow() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MAX_VALUE and the other is 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the specific edge case of overflowing from the maximum positive value to the minimum negative value.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMinPlusOne() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MIN_VALUE and the other is 1, then the method should return -2147483647. This tests correct arithmetic near the lower boundary without wrapping around.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(-2147483647, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testAddMinAndMax() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MIN_VALUE and the other is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return -1. This tests the interaction between the extreme boundaries of the integer range.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void testCommutativityOfAddition() {
        // If the method is called with arguments (a, b) and then with arguments (b, a) where a != b, then both calls should return the same result. This verifies that the implementation is commutative, which is a fundamental property of addition.
        int a = 3;
        int b = 7;
        int result1 = calculator.add(a, b);
        int result2 = calculator.add(b, a);
        assertEquals(result1, result2);
    }


}
package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void testAddPositiveIntegers() {
        // If two positive integers are provided as arguments (e.g., 5 and 10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (15). This tests the basic functionality described in 'Returns the sum of a and b'.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 10);
        assertEquals(15, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddNegativeIntegers() {
        // If two negative integers are provided as arguments (e.g., -5 and -10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-15). This ensures the method handles negative numbers correctly as part of the general 'sum' operation.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, -10);
        assertEquals(-15, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMixedSigns() {
        // If one positive integer and one negative integer are provided as arguments (e.g., 10 and -5), then the method should return their algebraic sum (5). This verifies correct handling of mixed signs.
        int result = calculator.add(10, -5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddZeroToZero() {
        // If both arguments are zero, then the method should return 0. This tests the identity element of addition.
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddNonZeroToZero() {
        // If one argument is non-zero (e.g., 5) and the other is zero, then the method should return the non-zero argument (5). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMaxIntegersOverflow() {
        // If both arguments are Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return the result of standard Java integer addition with overflow (specifically -2). Since the documentation does not specify throwing an exception for overflow, the test assumes standard two's complement wrap-around behavior.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMinIntegersOverflow() {
        // If both arguments are Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return the result of standard Java integer addition with overflow (specifically 0). This verifies consistent handling of minimum boundary values under overflow conditions.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMaxPlusOneOverflow() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MAX_VALUE and the other is 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the specific edge case of overflowing from the maximum positive value to the minimum negative value.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMinPlusOne() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MIN_VALUE and the other is 1, then the method should return -2147483647. This tests correct arithmetic near the lower boundary without wrapping around.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(-2147483647, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testAddMinAndMax() {
        // If one argument is Integer.MIN_VALUE and the other is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return -1. This tests the interaction between the extreme boundaries of the integer range.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void testCommutativityOfAddition() {
        // If the method is called with arguments (a, b) and then with arguments (b, a) where a != b, then both calls should return the same result. This verifies that the implementation is commutative, which is a fundamental property of addition.
        int a = 3;
        int b = 7;
        int result1 = calculator.add(a, b);
        int result2 = calculator.add(b, a);
        assertEquals(result1, result2);
    }
}

    15	        int result = calculator.add(5, 10);
    16	        assertEquals(15, result);
    17	    }

    23	        int result = calculator.add(-5, -10);
    24	        assertEquals(-15, result);
    25	    }

    31	        int result = calculator.add(10, -5);
    32	        assertEquals(5, result);
    33	    }

    49	    }

    55	        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);

    63	        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE);

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_numbers() {
        // Verify that subtracting a smaller positive integer from a larger positive integer returns the correct positive difference. If a=10 and b=5, then the result should be 5. This tests the core arithmetic behavior for standard positive inputs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_negative_numbers() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from another negative integer returns the correct result. If a=-10 and b=-5, then the result should be -5. This tests the behavior with negative operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-10, -5);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_mixed_signs_positive_minus_negative() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from a positive integer is equivalent to addition. If a=10 and b=-5, then the result should be 15. This tests the mathematical identity a - (-b) = a + b.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, -5);
        assertEquals(15, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_mixed_signs_positive_minus_larger() {
        // Verify that subtracting a larger positive integer from a smaller positive integer returns the correct negative difference. If a=5 and b=10, then the result should be -5. This tests the behavior when the result is negative.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 10);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_positive() {
        // Verify that subtracting a positive integer from zero returns the negative of that integer. If a=0 and b=5, then the result should be -5. This tests the edge case where the minuend is zero.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 5);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_any() {
        // Verify that subtracting zero from any integer returns that integer unchanged. If a=5 and b=0, then the result should be 5. This tests the identity property of subtraction with zero.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_zero() {
        // Verify that subtracting zero from zero returns zero. If a=0 and b=0, then the result should be 0. This tests the identity element for both operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_overflow_max_minus_negative() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative one from Integer.MAX_VALUE results in overflow wrapping to Integer.MIN_VALUE. If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=-1, then the result should be Integer.MIN_VALUE (-2147483648). This tests Java's two's complement wrap-around behavior on overflow, as no exception handling is specified in documentation.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_underflow_min_minus_negative() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative one from Integer.MIN_VALUE results in underflow wrapping to Integer.MAX_VALUE. If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=-1, then the result should be Integer.MAX_VALUE (2147483647). This tests Java's two's complement wrap-around behavior on underflow.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_minus_min_value() {
        // Verify that subtracting Integer.MIN_VALUE from zero results in overflow wrapping to Integer.MIN_VALUE. If a=0 and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the result should be Integer.MIN_VALUE (-2147483648). This tests a specific overflow case where the mathematical result (2147483648) exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


}
package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_numbers() {
        // Verify that subtracting a smaller positive integer from a larger positive integer returns the correct positive difference. If a=10 and b=5, then the result should be 5. This tests the core arithmetic behavior for standard positive inputs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_negative_numbers() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from another negative integer returns the correct result. If a=-10 and b=-5, then the result should be -5. This tests the behavior with negative operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-10, -5);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_mixed_signs_positive_minus_negative() {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from a positive integer is equivalent to addition. If a=10 and b=-5, then the result should be 15. This tests the mathematical identity a - (-b) = a + b.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, -5);
        assertEquals(15, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_mixed_signs_positive_minus_larger() {
        // Verify that subtracting a larger positive integer from a smaller positive integer returns the correct negative difference. If a=5 and b=10, then the result should be -5. This tests the behavior when the result is negative.

        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 10);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test

        // Verify that subtracting a positive integer from zero returns the negative of that integer. If a=0 and b=5, then the result should be -5. This tests the edge case where the minuend is zero.

        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test

        // Verify that subtracting zero from zero returns zero. If a=0 and b=0, then the result should be 0. This tests the identity element for both operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test

public class Calculator {

    /**
     * Returns the sum of a and b.

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertTimeoutPreemptively;

import java.time.Duration;
import java.lang.reflect.Field;
import java.util.Arrays;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void testDummy2CompletesWithoutException() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked on a valid instance of the class, then the method must complete execution without throwing any checked or unchecked exceptions. This verifies the core behavior that the method 'does nothing' and does not crash.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        calculator.dummy2();
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2HasNoSideEffectsOnInstanceState() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked, then the internal state of the instance (all fields) must remain unchanged compared to the state before the invocation. Since the documentation states it 'has no code' and 'does nothing', it must not modify any instance variables.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        
        // Capture the state of all fields before invocation
        Class<?> clazz = Calculator.class;
        Field[] fields = clazz.getDeclaredFields();
        Object[] statesBefore = new Object[fields.length];
        
        try {
            for (int i = 0; i < fields.length; i++) {
                fields[i].setAccessible(true);
                statesBefore[i] = fields[i].get(calculator);
            }
            
            // Invoke the method
            calculator.dummy2();
            
            // Verify that all fields remain unchanged
            for (int i = 0; i < fields.length; i++) {
                Object stateAfter = fields[i].get(calculator);
                if (statesBefore[i] == null) {
                    assertEquals(null, stateAfter, "Field " + fields[i].getName() + " was modified to a non-null value");
                } else {
                    assertEquals(statesBefore[i], stateAfter, "Field " + fields[i].getName() + " was modified");
                }
            }
        } catch (IllegalAccessException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException("Failed to access fields for state comparison", e);
        }
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2HasNoSideEffectsOnStaticState() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked, then no static fields of the class or other global states are modified. This ensures the method adheres to the 'does nothing' contract regarding broader system state.
        Class<?> clazz = Calculator.class;
        Field[] staticFields = Arrays.stream(clazz.getDeclaredFields())
                .filter(f -> java.lang.reflect.Modifier.isStatic(f.getModifiers()))
                .toArray(Field[]::new);
        
        Object[] statesBefore = new Object[staticFields.length];
        
        try {
            for (int i = 0; i < staticFields.length; i++) {
                staticFields[i].setAccessible(true);
                statesBefore[i] = staticFields[i].get(null);
            }
            
            // Invoke the method on a new instance
            Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
            calculator.dummy2();
            
            // Verify that all static fields remain unchanged
            for (int i = 0; i < staticFields.length; i++) {
                Object stateAfter = staticFields[i].get(null);
                if (statesBefore[i] == null) {
                    assertEquals(null, stateAfter, "Static field " + staticFields[i].getName() + " was modified to a non-null value");
                } else {
                    assertEquals(statesBefore[i], stateAfter, "Static field " + staticFields[i].getName() + " was modified");
                }
            }
        } catch (IllegalAccessException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException("Failed to access static fields for state comparison", e);
        }
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2ReturnsImmediately() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked, then the method must return control to the caller immediately without blocking or entering an infinite loop. This aligns with the documentation stating it has 'no code' to execute.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        // Set a reasonable timeout - if the method has no code, it should complete very quickly
        assertTimeoutPreemptively(Duration.ofMillis(100), () -> {
            calculator.dummy2();
        });
    }
